See also: [Killing of Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Osama_bin_Laden "Killing of Osama bin Laden") and [Coalition combat operations in Afghanistan in 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_combat_operations_in_Afghanistan_in_2010 "Coalition combat operations in Afghanistan in 2010")

Deployment of additional US troops continued in early 2010, with 9,000 of the planned 30,000 in place before the end of March and another 18,000 expected by June.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-247) The surge in troops supported a sixfold increase in Special Forces operations.[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Eric_Schmitt-248) The surge of American personnel that began in late 2009 ended by September 2012.[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-249) 700 airstrikes occurred in September 2010 alone versus 257 in all of 2009.[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-250)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Royal_Air_Force_Regiment_in_Afghanistan.JPG/500px-Royal_Air_Force_Regiment_in_Afghanistan.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Royal_Air_Force_Regiment_in_Afghanistan.JPG) UK service members of the [Royal Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Air_Force "Royal Air Force") Regiment stop on a road while conducting a combat mission near Kandahar Airfield, 2010

Due to increased use of IEDs by insurgents, the number of injured Coalition soldiers, mainly Americans, significantly increased.[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-251) Beginning in May 2010, NATO special forces began to concentrate on operations to capture or kill specific Taliban leaders. As of March 2011, the US military claimed that the effort had resulted in the capture or killing of more than 900 low- to mid-level Taliban commanders.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-USAToday20110703-252)[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-253) Overall, 2010 saw the most insurgent attacks of any year since the war began, peaking in September at more than 1,500.[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-254)

In February 2010, Coalition and Afghan forces began highly visible plans for an offensive, codenamed [Operation Moshtarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Moshtarak "Operation Moshtarak"), on a Taliban stronghold near the village of [Marjah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marjah "Marjah").[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-255)

The ["Peace Jirga"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Peace_Jirga_2010 "Afghan Peace Jirga 2010") was held in Kabul, attended by 1,600 delegates, in June 2010. However, the Taliban and the [Hezb-i Islami Gulbuddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezb-i_Islami_Gulbuddin "Hezb-i Islami Gulbuddin"), who were both invited by Karzai as a gesture of goodwill did not attend the conference.[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-256) The Taliban's co-founder and then-second-in-command, [Abdul Ghani Baradar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Ghani_Baradar "Abdul Ghani Baradar"), was one of the leading Taliban members who favored talks with the US and Afghan governments. Karzai's administration reportedly held talks with Baradar in February; however, later that month, Baradar was captured in a joint US-Pakistani raid in [Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi "Karachi"), Pakistan. The arrest infuriated Karzai and invoked suspicions that he was seized because the [Pakistani intelligence community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_intelligence_community "Pakistani intelligence community") was opposed to Afghan peace talks.[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-257)[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-258) Karzai started peace talks with Haqqani Network groups in March.[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-259)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/ASLAV_in_Afghanistan_during_early_2011.jpg/500px-ASLAV_in_Afghanistan_during_early_2011.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ASLAV_in_Afghanistan_during_early_2011.jpg) An Australian service light armored vehicle drives through [Tangi Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangi_Valley "Tangi Valley"), 2011

In 2010, the Obama administration decided to allow possible political negotiations to solve the war.[\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-260) The Taliban themselves had refused to speak to the Afghan government, portraying them as an American "puppet." Sporadic efforts for peace talks between the US and the Taliban occurred afterward, and it was reported in October that Taliban leadership commanders (the " [Quetta Shura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quetta_Shura "Quetta Shura")") had left their haven in Pakistan and been safely escorted to Kabul by NATO aircraft for talks, with the assurance that NATO staff would not apprehend them.[\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-261) After the talks concluded, it emerged that the leader of this delegation, who claimed to be [Akhtar Mansour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhtar_Mansour "Akhtar Mansour"), the second-in-command of the Taliban, was actually an imposter who had duped NATO officials.[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-262)

On 25 July 2010, the [release of 91,731 classified documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_War_documents_leak "Afghan War documents leak") from the [WikiLeaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WikiLeaks "WikiLeaks") organization was made public. The documents cover US military incident and intelligence reports from January 2004 to December 2009.[\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-263) Some of these documents included sanitized, and "covered up", accounts of civilian casualties caused by [Coalition Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Forces "Coalition Forces"). The reports included many references to other incidents involving civilian casualties like the Kunduz airstrike and [Nangar Khel incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nangar_Khel_incident "Nangar Khel incident").[\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-264) The leaked documents also contain reports of Pakistan collusion with the Taliban. According to _Der Spiegel_, "the documents clearly show that the Pakistani intelligence agency [Inter-Services Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") (ISI) is the most important accomplice the Taliban has outside of Afghanistan."[\[263\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Spiegel2-265)

On 2 May 2011, US officials announced that bin Laden had been killed in [Operation Neptune Spear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Neptune_Spear "Operation Neptune Spear"), conducted by the US Navy SEALs in [Abbottabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbottabad "Abbottabad"), Pakistan.[\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-266) Pakistan came under intense international scrutiny after the raid. The Pakistani government denied that it had sheltered bin Laden, and said it had shared information with the CIA and other intelligence agencies about the compound since 2009.[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-267)

The 2011 [Battle of Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kandahar_(2011) "Battle of Kandahar (2011)") was part of an offensive that followed a 30 April announcement that the Taliban would launch their spring offensive.[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-268) On 7 May, the Taliban launched a major offensive on government buildings in Kandahar.[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-269) The [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") called it "the worst attack in Kandahar Province since \[2001\], and an embarrassment for the \[Afghan\] government."[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-270)

Karzai confirmed in June 2011 that secret talks were taking place between the US and the Taliban,[\[269\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-271) but these collapsed by August.[\[270\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-272)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Bundeswehr_in_Afghanistan%2C_August_2011.jpg/500px-Bundeswehr_in_Afghanistan%2C_August_2011.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bundeswehr_in_Afghanistan,_August_2011.jpg) A German [Bundeswehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundeswehr "Bundeswehr") soldier, part of ISAF's Regional Command North at [Camp Marmal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Marmal "Camp Marmal"), 2011

On 22 June 2011, Obama announced that 10,000 troops [would be withdrawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Afghanistan_(2011%E2%80%932016) "Withdrawal of United States troops from Afghanistan (2011–2016)") by the end of the year, and an additional 23,000 troops would return by the summer of 2012. After the withdrawal of 10,000 US troops, 80,000 remained.[\[271\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-273) In 2011, Canada withdrew its combat troops, transitioning to a training role. Following suit, other NATO countries announced troop reductions. Taliban attacks continued at the same rate as they did in 2011, around 28,000 in 2013.[\[272\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-274)

Tensions between Pakistan and the US increased in late September 2011, after several Pakistan Frontier Corps soldiers were killed and wounded. The troops were attacked by a US piloted aircraft that was pursuing Taliban forces near the Afghan-Pakistan border, but for unknown reasons, opened fire on two Pakistan border posts. In retaliation, Pakistan closed the Torkham ground border crossing to NATO supply convoys for an unspecified period. This incident followed the release of a video allegedly showing uniformed Pakistan soldiers executing unarmed civilians.[\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-275) After the border closing, Pakistani Taliban attacked NATO convoys, killing several drivers, and destroying around 100 tankers.[\[274\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-276) ISAF forces [skirmished Pakistan's armed forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_NATO_attack_in_Pakistan "2011 NATO attack in Pakistan") on 26 November, killing 24 Pakistani soldiers. Each side claimed the other shot first. Pakistan blocked NATO supply lines and ordered Americans to leave [Shamsi Airfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamsi_Airfield "Shamsi Airfield").[\[275\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-The_News-277)[\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Newsweek-012-278)

### 2012–2013: US troop incidents, Obama-Karzai meetings

See also: [US–Afghanistan Strategic Partnership Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93Afghanistan_Strategic_Partnership_Agreement "US–Afghanistan Strategic Partnership Agreement")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/U.S._Soldiers_walk_by_two_Afghan_boys_while_performing_a_presence_patrol_through_the_snow-covered_streets_of_Gardez%2C_Paktia_province%2C_Afghanistan%2C_Feb_120216-A-ZU930-005.jpg/500px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:U.S._Soldiers_walk_by_two_Afghan_boys_while_performing_a_presence_patrol_through_the_snow-covered_streets_of_Gardez,_Paktia_province,_Afghanistan,_Feb_120216-A-ZU930-005.jpg) US Army Soldiers walk by local Afghan boys during a patrol in [Gardez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gardez "Gardez"), 2012

[Beginning in January 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_of_US_troops_urinating_on_Taliban_fighters "Video of US troops urinating on Taliban fighters"), incidents involving US troops[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-SMH20120419-279)[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-NYT_20120418_2-280)[\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-RHP20120418-281)[\[280\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-282)[\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-CNN20120418-283)[\[282\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-284) occurred that were described by _[The Sydney Morning Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sydney_Morning_Herald "The Sydney Morning Herald")_ as "a series of damaging incidents and disclosures involving US troops in Afghanistan."[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-SMH20120419-279) These incidents fractured the partnership between Afghanistan and ISAF,[\[283\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-285) raised the question whether discipline within US troops was breaking down,[\[284\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-286) undermined "the image of foreign forces in a country where there is already deep resentment owing to civilian deaths and a perception among many Afghans that US troops lack respect for Afghan culture and people"[\[285\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Guardian20120706-287) and strained [relations between Afghanistan and the US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93United_States_relations "Afghanistan–United States relations").[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-NYT_20120418_2-280)[\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-RHP20120418-281) Besides an [incident involving US troops who posed with body parts of dead insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgents'_bodies_incident "Insurgents' bodies incident") and a video apparently showing a US helicopter crew blasting a group of Afghan men with a Hellfire missile,[\[285\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Guardian20120706-287)[\[286\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-288) these "high-profile US military incidents in Afghanistan"[\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-CNN20120418-283) also included the [2012 Afghanistan Quran burning protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Afghanistan_Quran_burning_protests "2012 Afghanistan Quran burning protests") and the [Panjwai shooting spree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar_massacre "Kandahar massacre").

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Flickr_-_The_U.S._Army_-_Black_Hawk_boarding_%281%29.jpg/500px-Flickr_-_The_U.S._Army_-_Black_Hawk_boarding_%281%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Flickr_-_The_U.S._Army_-_Black_Hawk_boarding_(1).jpg) US Army soldiers boarding a [Black Hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikorsky_UH-60_Black_Hawk "Sikorsky UH-60 Black Hawk") in [Nari District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nari_District "Nari District"), near the Pakistani border, 2012

Karzai visited the US in January 2012. At the time, the US stated its openness to withdrawing all of its troops by the end of 2014.[\[287\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-289) Karzai and Obama agreed to transfer combat operations from NATO to Afghan forces by spring 2013 rather than summer 2013.[\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-NYTO20130111-290)[\[289\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-CNN20130112-291) "What's going to happen this spring is that Afghans will be in the lead throughout the country", Obama said. "They \[ISAF forces\] will still be fighting alongside Afghan troops...we will be in a training, assisting, advising role."[\[289\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-CNN20130112-291) He also stated the reason of the withdrawals that "We achieved our central goal, or have come very close...which is to de-capacitate al-Qaeda" and making sure that "they can't attack us again."[\[290\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-WP20130111-292) He added that any US mission beyond 2014 would focus solely on [counterterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterterrorism "Counterterrorism") operations and training.[\[290\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-WP20130111-292)[\[291\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-LATimes20130111-293)

In 2012, the leaders of NATO-member countries endorsed an exit strategy during the NATO Summit.[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-dx-154) ISAF Forces would transfer command of all combat missions to Afghan forces by the middle of 2013,[\[292\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Reuters_20120521-294) while shifting from combat to advising, training and assisting [Afghan security forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_National_Security_Forces "Afghan National Security Forces").[\[293\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Chicago_Declaration-295)[\[294\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-296) Most of the 130,000 ISAF troops would depart by the end of December 2014,[\[292\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Reuters_20120521-294) and a new NATO mission would then assume the support role.[\[293\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Chicago_Declaration-295)[\[295\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-297)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Humvees%2C_in_winter%2C_in_Afghanistan_-_120125-A-0000F-006.jpg/500px-Humvees%2C_in_winter%2C_in_Afghanistan_-_120125-A-0000F-006.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Humvees,_in_winter,_in_Afghanistan_-_120125-A-0000F-006.jpg) Troops from the 31st and 33rd Kandak, Afghan National Army, execute a departure for Operation Valley Flood, 2012

Further attempts to resume talks were canceled in March 2012[\[296\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-298) and June 2013, following a dispute between Afghanistan and the Taliban regarding the latter's opening of a [political office in Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_activities_in_Qatar "Taliban activities in Qatar"). Karzai accused the Taliban of portraying themselves as a [government-in-exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_in_exile "Government in exile").[\[297\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-299)

On [2 May 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Chicago_summit "2012 Chicago summit"), Karzai and Obama signed [a strategic partnership agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93Afghanistan_Strategic_Partnership_Agreement "US–Afghanistan Strategic Partnership Agreement") between the two countries, after the latter had arrived unannounced in Kabul.[\[298\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-NYT20120501-300) On 7 July, as part of the agreement, the US designated Afghanistan a [major non-NATO ally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_non-NATO_ally "Major non-NATO ally") after Karzai and Clinton met in Kabul.[\[299\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-301) Both leaders agreed that the US would transfer Afghan prisoners and prisons to the Afghan government[\[289\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-CNN20130112-291)[\[300\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-BBC20130111-302) and withdraw troops from Afghan villages in spring 2013.[\[300\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-BBC20130111-302)[\[301\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-NYT20130111-303)

On 18 June 2013, the transfer of security responsibilities from NATO to Afghan forces was completed.[\[302\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-304) ISAF remained slated to end its mission by the end of 2014.[\[303\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-WSJ29130618-305) Some 100,000 ISAF forces remained in the country.[\[304\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-AJE20130618-306)

### 2014–2015: Withdrawal and increase of insurgency

See also: [Operation Freedom's Sentinel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Freedom's_Sentinel "Operation Freedom's Sentinel")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Presentation_of_the_Resolute_Support_Colors.jpg/500px-Presentation_of_the_Resolute_Support_Colors.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Presentation_of_the_Resolute_Support_Colors.jpg) Resolute Support Colors presented at Kabul on 28 December 2014, after the ISAF colors are encased

The UK and the US officially ended their combat operation in Afghanistan on 26 October 2014. The UK handed over its last base in Afghanistan, [Camp Bastion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Shorabak "Camp Shorabak"), and the US handed over its last base, [Camp Leatherneck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Leatherneck "Camp Leatherneck"), to Afghan forces.[\[305\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-307) Around 500 UK troops remained in "non-combat" roles.[\[306\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-308)[\[307\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-309) On 28 December, NATO officially ended combat operations in a ceremony held in Kabul.[\[308\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-2014APcessation-310) Continued operations by US forces within Afghanistan were under [Operation Freedom's Sentinel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Freedom's_Sentinel "Operation Freedom's Sentinel");[\[309\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-311) and the new NATO mission was [Operation Resolute Support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Resolute_Support "Operation Resolute Support").[\[310\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-312) The withdrawal of troops did not mean the withdrawal of military presence. As US troops withdrew from Afghanistan, they were replaced by [military contractors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_military_company "Private military company") hired by the US and UN. Many of these contractors consisted of ex-Coalition military personnel. This allowed the US and British to continue to be involved in ground actions without the requirement to station their own forces.[\[311\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-313)

The Taliban began a resurgence due to several factors. The withdrawal of most foreign forces from Afghanistan reduced the risk the Taliban faced of being bombed and raided. In June 2014, Pakistan launched [Operation Zarb-e-Azb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Zarb-e-Azb "Operation Zarb-e-Azb") in the [North Waziristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Waziristan "North Waziristan") tribal area, and dislodged thousands of mainly Uzbek, Arab, and Pakistani militants, who flooded into Afghanistan and swelled the Taliban's ranks. The group was further emboldened by the relative lack of interest from the international community, as attention was given to crises in [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Civil_War "Syrian Civil War"), [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Civil_War_(2014%E2%80%932017) "Iraqi Civil War (2014–2017)"), and [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_(2014%E2%80%932022) "War in Donbas (2014–2022)"). Afghan security forces lacked, among other things, air power and reconnaissance. The political infighting in Kabul, and the apparent weakness in governance at different levels, were exploited by the Taliban.[\[312\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-314) The Taliban expanded governance in the areas under their control, attempting to build local-level legitimacy.[\[313\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-315) Their governance strategy rested in particular on the provision of justice, which was often viewed as less corrupt than the courts of the government.[\[314\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-316)[\[315\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-317)

Heavy fighting occurred in Kunduz Province,[\[316\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-318)[\[317\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-319) which was the site of [clashes from 2009 onwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterinsurgency_in_Northern_Afghanistan "Counterinsurgency in Northern Afghanistan"). In May 2015, flights into the Kunduz city were suspended due to weeks of clashes between the Afghan security forces and the Taliban outside the city.[\[318\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-320) The intensifying conflict in the Northern [Char Dara District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Char_Dara_District "Char Dara District") within Kunduz Province led the Afghan government to enlist local militia fighters to bolster opposition to the Taliban insurgency.[\[319\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-321) In June, the Taliban intensified attacks around [Kunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz "Kunduz") city as part of a major offensive to try and capture it;[\[320\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-322)[\[321\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-323)[\[322\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-324) tens of thousands of inhabitants were displaced internally. The government recaptured the Char Dara district after roughly a month of fighting.[\[323\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-325)

In late September, Taliban forces launched an attack on Kunduz city, seizing outlying villages and entering the city. The Taliban stormed the regional hospital and clashed with security forces at the nearby university. The fighting saw the Taliban attack from four different districts: Char Dara to the west, Aliabad to the southwest, Khanabad to the east, and Imam Saheb to the north.[\[324\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Guardian_Kunduz-326)[\[325\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-327) The Taliban took the Zakhel and Ali Khel villages on the highway leading south, which connects the city to Kabul and Mazar-e Sharif through Aliabad district. They reportedly made their largest gains in the southwest of Kunduz, where some armed local communities had started supporting the Taliban.[\[324\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Guardian_Kunduz-326) Taliban fighters had allegedly blocked the route to the airport, to prevent civilians fleeing the city.[\[326\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-328) One witness reported that the headquarters of the [National Directorate of Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Directorate_of_Security "National Directorate of Security") was set on fire.[\[327\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-329)

In January 2015, the [Islamic State caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State "Islamic State") (IS) established a branch in Afghanistan called [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State "Islamic State") (ISIS-K), and began recruiting fighters[\[328\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-ISIL_confirmed_to_have_presence_in_Afghanistan-330) and clashing with the Taliban.[\[329\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-cbsnews.com1-331)[\[330\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-332) It was created after pledging allegiance to the self-assumed worldwide caliph [Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi "Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi").[\[331\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-333) On 18 March, Hafiz Wahidi, IS' replacement deputy Emir in Afghanistan, was killed by the Afghan Armed Forces, along with 9 other IS militants accompanying him.[\[332\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-ISIL_Afghan_replacement_deputy_killed-334)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/TAAC-E_advisers_emphasize_Afghan_police_logistics_in_Nangarhar_150106-A-VO006-013.jpg/500px-TAAC-E_advisers_emphasize_Afghan_police_logistics_in_Nangarhar_150106-A-VO006-013.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TAAC-E_advisers_emphasize_Afghan_police_logistics_in_Nangarhar_150106-A-VO006-013.jpg) US Army soldier in Nangarhar Province, 2015

In 2015, the Taliban began an offensive that took over parts of Helmand Province. By June, they had seized control of [Dishu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dishu_District "Dishu District") and [Baghran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghran_District "Baghran District") killing 5,588 Afghan government security forces (3,720 of them were police officers).[\[333\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-335) By the end of July, the Taliban had overrun [Nawzad District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawzad_District "Nawzad District")[\[334\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-336) and on 26 August, the Taliban took control of [Musa Qala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musa_Qala_District "Musa Qala District").[\[335\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-337) In October, Taliban forces had attempted to take [Lashkar Gah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lashkar_Gah "Lashkar Gah"), the capital of Helmand province. The Afghan 215th Corps and special operations forces launched a counteroffensive against the Taliban in November,[\[336\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-338) Whilst the assault was repelled, Taliban forces remained dug into the city's suburbs as of December 2015.[\[337\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Prepare_200_Graves_Times-339)

On 22 June 2015, the Taliban detonated a car bomb outside the National Assembly in Kabul, and attacked the building with assault rifles and [RPGs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket-propelled_grenade "Rocket-propelled grenade").[\[338\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-CNN_June2015-340)[\[339\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Reuters_June2015-341) The bombing highlighted differences within the Taliban in their approach to peace talks.[\[340\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-economistjune-342)[\[341\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-343)

In July 2015, Pakistan hosted the first official peace talks between Taliban representatives and the Afghan government. The US and China attended the talks brokered by Pakistan in Murree as two observers.[\[342\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-344) In January 2016, Pakistan hosted a round of four-way talks with Afghan, Chinese and American officials, but the Taliban did not attend.[\[343\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-345) The Taliban did hold informal talks with the Afghan government in 2016.[\[344\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-346) China's reason for the negotiation was that Afghan security situation affected its own separatist groups, and economic activity with Pakistan. The Taliban declined.[\[345\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-347)[\[346\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Aljazeera-348)

On 11 November 2015, it was reported that in Zabul Province, Taliban fighters loyal to the new Taliban leader Mullah Akhtar Mansoor fought a pro-IS splinter faction of the Taliban led by Mullah Mansoor Dadullah. Even though Dadullah's faction enjoyed the support of foreign IS fighters, including Uzbeks and Chechens, Mansoor's loyalists reportedly had the upper hand. According to a Zabul Province official, more than 100 militants from both sides were killed in the conflict.[\[347\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-349) The infighting stifled peace talks.[\[348\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-the_guardian-350)[\[349\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-US_department_of_defence-351)

The infighting caused Mansoor to be consumed with a campaign to quell dissent against his leadership; this led [Sirajuddin Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirajuddin_Haqqani "Sirajuddin Haqqani"), then-chief of the Haqqani Network, to be selected as the deputy leader of the Taliban in summer 2015. Sirajuddin and other Haqqani leaders increasingly ran the Taliban's day-to-day military operations, notably organizing urban terrorist attacks, and building a complex international fundraising network. They also appointed Taliban governors, and began uniting the Taliban. As a result, the Haqqani Network, mostly autonomous until then, became deeply integrated with the Taliban, and grew in influence within the insurgency. Tensions with the Pakistani military grew because American and Afghan officials accused them of sheltering the Haqqanis as a proxy group.[\[350\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-nytimes.com_2-352)[\[351\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-ABC_news-353)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/TAAC-E_advisers_observe_progress_in_Afghan_police_logistics_150217-A-VO006-028.jpg/500px-TAAC-E_advisers_observe_progress_in_Afghan_police_logistics_150217-A-VO006-028.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TAAC-E_advisers_observe_progress_in_Afghan_police_logistics_150217-A-VO006-028.jpg) [TAAC-E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Train_Advise_Assist_Command_%E2%80%93_East "Train Advise Assist Command – East") advisers in 2015

December 2015 saw a renewed Taliban offensive in Helmand focused on the town of [Sangin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangin "Sangin"). The Sangin district fell to the Taliban on 21 December after fierce clashes that killed more than 90 soldiers in two days.[\[352\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-354) It was reported that 30 members of the [SAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Air_Service "Special Air Service"), alongside 60 US special forces operators, joined the Afghan Army in the battle to retake parts of Sangin from Taliban insurgents.[\[353\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-355) In addition, about 300 US troops and a small number of British remained in Helmand to advise Afghan commanders at the [corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corps "Corps") level.[\[354\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-auto2017-356)[\[355\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-357) Senior American commanders said that the Afghan troops in the province had lacked effective leaders, as well as the necessary weapons and ammunition to hold off persistent Taliban attacks. Some Afghan soldiers in Helmand had fought in tough conditions for years without getting to see their families, causing poor morale and high desertion.[\[354\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-auto2017-356)

Peace movements started arising in Afghanistan, including the [Tabassum movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabassum_movement "Tabassum movement") in 2015, the [Enlightenment Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlightenment_Movement_(Afghanistan) "Enlightenment Movement (Afghanistan)") during 2016–17, [Uprising for Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uprising_for_Change_(Afghanistan) "Uprising for Change (Afghanistan)") in 2017, and the People's Peace Movement in 2018.

### 2016–2017: Collapse of peace talks, emergence of Islamic State

See also: [Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State "Islamic State")

In January 2016, the US government granted the Pentagon new legal authority for a US offensive against ISIS-K-affiliated militants, following the State Department designating IS in Afghanistan and Pakistan as a foreign terrorist organization. The number of militants started with around 60 or 70, with most of them coming over the Pakistani border, but eventually\[ _[when?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_\] ranged between 1,000 and 3,000 militants.[\[356\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-bbc.co.uk-358) They were mainly defectors from the Afghan and the Pakistani Taliban, and were generally confined to [Nangarhar Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nangarhar_Province "Nangarhar Province"), and partially, Kunar Province.[\[356\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-bbc.co.uk-358)[\[357\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-359)

In early February 2016, Taliban insurgents renewed their assault on Sangin, after previously being repulsed in December 2015, launching many strong attacks on Afghan government forces earlier in the month. As a result, the US sent troops from the 2nd Battalion, [87th Infantry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/87th_Infantry_Regiment_(United_States) "87th Infantry Regiment (United States)"), and [10th Mountain Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_Mountain_Division "10th Mountain Division") to prop up the Afghan [215th Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/215th_Corps_(Afghanistan) "215th Corps (Afghanistan)") in Helmand Province, particularly around Sangin, joining US special ops forces already in the area.[\[358\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-360)[\[359\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-361)[\[360\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-362)[\[361\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-363)[\[362\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-the_New_York_Times-364) On 14 March 2016, [Khanneshin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanneshin "Khanneshin") District in Helmand Province fell to the Taliban; and district by district, Afghan troops were retreating back to urban centers in Helmand.[\[346\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Aljazeera-348)[\[362\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-the_New_York_Times-364) In early April 2016, 600 Afghan troops launched a major offensive to retake Taliban-occupied areas in and around Sangin.[\[363\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-365) An Afghan army offensive to retake the town of Khanisheen was repelled by the Taliban, and desertions in the army were rife.[\[364\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-366)

Despite US airstrikes, militants besieged Lashkar Gah, reportedly controlling all roads leading to the city and areas a few kilometres away. The US stepped up airstrikes in support of Afghan ground forces. Afghan forces in the city were reported as "exhausted", whilst police checkpoints around the capital were falling one by one. Meanwhile, the Taliban sent a new elite commando force into Helmand called " [Sara Khitta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Group "Red Group")" in Pashto.[\[365\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-367)[\[366\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-368)[\[367\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-369) Afghan security forces repelled attacks by Taliban fighters encroaching on Chah-e-Anji nearby Lashkar Gah; Afghan special forces backed by US airstrikes battled increasingly well-armed and disciplined Taliban militants. An Afghan special forces commander said: "The Taliban have heavily armed, uniformed units that are equipped with night vision and modern weapons."[\[368\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-370)

On 10 March 2016, officials said that the Taliban clashed with a Taliban splinter group (led by Muhammad Rasul) in the Shindand district of [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat"), and up to 100 militants were killed.[\[348\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-the_guardian-350)[\[349\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-US_department_of_defence-351)

In April 2016, Afghan president [Ashraf Ghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Ghani "Ashraf Ghani") ended his' governments failing effort to start peace talks with the Taliban.[\[369\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-371) Additionally, due to the integration of Haqqani Networks into the Taliban leadership, it would become harder for peace talks to take place.[\[350\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-nytimes.com_2-352)[\[351\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-ABC_news-353) Although leader of the Taliban, [Haibatullah Akhundzada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hibatullah_Akhundzada "Hibatullah Akhundzada"), said a peace agreement was possible if the Kabul government renounced its foreign allies.[\[370\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-372)

On 23 July 2016, Afghan and US forces began an offensive to clear Nangarhar Province of IS militants hours after the [Kabul bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2016_Kabul_bombing "July 2016 Kabul bombing"). The operation was dubbed "Wrath of the Storm", involving both Afghan regular army and special forces, and was the Afghan army's first major offensive that summer. The estimated size of ISIS-K in January 2016 was around 3,000, but by July, it had dropped to around 1,000 to 1,500, with 70% of its fighters coming from the TTP.[\[371\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-militarytimes-373)[\[372\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-374)[\[373\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-375)

As of July 2016, at least an estimated 20% of Afghanistan was under Taliban control, with southernmost [Helmand Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmand_Province "Helmand Province") as a major stronghold,[\[374\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-376) while [General John Nicholson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_W._Nicholson_Jr. "John W. Nicholson Jr.") stated that Afghan Armed Forces' casualties had risen 20% compared to 2015.[\[371\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-militarytimes-373) On 22 August, the US announced that 100 US troops were sent to Lashkar Gah to help prevent the Taliban from overrunning it, in what Brigadier General Charles Cleveland called a "temporary effort" to advise Afghan police.[\[375\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-377)

On 22 September 2016, the Afghan government signed a draft peace deal with Hezb-i-Islami.[\[376\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-378)[\[377\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-379)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Soldiers_of_Special_Forces_of_10th_Special_Forces_Group_%28Airborne%29_memorialize_two_of_their_fallen_brothers_during_a_memorial_held_at_Kunduz_Airfield_in_Afghanistan_on_Nov._7%2C_2016.jpg/500px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Soldiers_of_Special_Forces_of_10th_Special_Forces_Group_(Airborne)_memorialize_two_of_their_fallen_brothers_during_a_memorial_held_at_Kunduz_Airfield_in_Afghanistan_on_Nov._7,_2016.jpg) Green Berets of the [10th SFG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10th_Special_Forces_Group_(United_States) "10th Special Forces Group (United States)") memorialize two comrades who were killed in action during the [Battle of Boz Qandahari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Boz_Qandahari "Battle of Boz Qandahari") in 2016

On 31 December 2016, the Taliban continued their assault on the province with attacks on Sangin and Marjah districts.[\[378\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-380) Some estimated suggest the Taliban had retaken more than 80% of Helmand province.[\[379\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-marinecorpstimes-381)

In early January 2017, the _Marine Corps Times_ reported that Afghan forces sought to rebuild, following an exhausting 2016 fighting season; 33 districts, spread across 16 Afghan provinces, were under insurgent control whilst 258 were under government control and nearly 120 districts remained "contested."[\[379\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-marinecorpstimes-381) The Afghan army reportedly had about 169,000 soldiers, but in 2016, suffered a 33% attrition rate—a 7% increase from 2015.[\[379\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-marinecorpstimes-381)

In early March 2017, American and Afghan forces launched Operation Hamza to "flush" ISIS-K from its stronghold in eastern Afghanistan, engaging in regular ground battles.[\[380\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-382) In April 2017, a NATO spokesman said that Afghan and international forces had reduced ISIS-K controlled territory in Afghanistan by two-thirds, and had killed around half their fighters in two years. Since the beginning of 2017, 460 airstrikes against terrorists (with drone strikes alone killing more than 200 IS militants); he added that the affiliate had an estimated 600–800 fighters in two eastern Afghan provinces.[\[381\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-383)

On 23 March 2017, [Sangin district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangin_District "Sangin District") was captured by the Taliban, as they had overrun the district center of the town of Sangin. During the earlier phase of the war, almost a quarter of British casualties were caused by fighting for the town, while more recently hundreds of Afghan troops died defending it.[\[382\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-384)[\[383\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-385) On 29 April, the US deployed an additional 5,000 Marines to southern Helmand Province.[\[384\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-386)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/U.S._Army_CH-47_Chinook_helicopter_pilots_fly_near_Jalalabad%2C_Afghanistan%2C_April_5%2C_2017.jpg/500px-U.S._Army_CH-47_Chinook_helicopter_pilots_fly_near_Jalalabad%2C_Afghanistan%2C_April_5%2C_2017.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:U.S._Army_CH-47_Chinook_helicopter_pilots_fly_near_Jalalabad,_Afghanistan,_April_5,_2017.jpg) US Army pilots fly a CH-47 Chinook in Nangarhar, 2017

On 21 April 2017, the Taliban [attacked Camp Shaheen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Camp_Shaheen_attack "2017 Camp Shaheen attack") near Mazar-e-Sharif, killing over 140 Afghan soldiers.[\[385\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-387)[\[386\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-388)[\[387\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-389)

On 15 September 2017, it was reported that the CIA was seeking authority to conduct its own [drone strikes in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drone_strikes_in_Afghanistan "Drone strikes in Afghanistan") and other war zones, according to current and former intelligence and military officials, and that the change in authority was being considered by the White House as part of the new strategy despite concerns by the Pentagon.[\[388\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-390) On 19 September, the [Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_presidency_of_Donald_Trump "First presidency of Donald Trump") deployed another 3,000 US troops to Afghanistan, adding to the roughly 11,000 US troops already there.[\[389\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-391) On 4 October, Defense Secretary [Jim Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Mattis "Jim Mattis") approved a change in [rules of engagement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rules_of_engagement "Rules of engagement") as part of the new strategy so that there was no longer a requirement for US troops to be in contact with enemy forces in Afghanistan before opening fire.[\[390\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-392)

On 20 November 2017, General Nicholson announced that US and Afghan airstrikes were targeting Taliban-run drug production facilities in Afghanistan, under a new strategy aimed at cutting off Taliban funding, saying that the latter was "becoming a criminal organization" that was earning about $200 million a year from drug-related activities. Ghani strongly endorsed the airstrikes.[\[391\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-393)

### 2018–2019: Peace overtures

Further information: [Afghan peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_peace_process "Afghan peace process"), [Battle of Darzab (2018)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Darzab_(2018) "Battle of Darzab (2018)"), [Ghazni offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni_offensive "Ghazni offensive"), and [2019 Afghan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Afghan_presidential_election "2019 Afghan presidential election")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Taliban_insurgency_in_Afghanistan_%282015%E2%80%932021%29.svg/960px-Taliban_insurgency_in_Afghanistan_%282015%E2%80%932021%29.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Taliban_insurgency_in_Afghanistan_(2015%E2%80%93present).svg) Map showing the war as of January 2019

Under control of the [Afghan Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Afghanistan "Politics of Afghanistan"), [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolute_Support_Mission "Resolute Support Mission"), and [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2015%E2%80%93present)#Allied_militias "War in Afghanistan (2015–present)")

Under control of the [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban"), [Al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda "Al-Qaeda"), and [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Jihad_Union "Islamic Jihad Union")

Under control of the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State "Islamic State") (ISIL) and [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Movement_of_Uzbekistan "Islamic Movement of Uzbekistan")

Under control of the [Pakistani Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Army "Pakistani Army")

In January 2018, the Taliban were openly active in 70% of the country (being in full control of 14 districts and have an active and open physical presence in a further 263) and IS was more active in the country than ever before. Following recent attacks by the Taliban (including the [Kabul ambulance bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_ambulance_bombing "Kabul ambulance bombing") on 27 January) and IS that killed scores of civilians, U.S. president [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") and Afghan officials decided to rule out any talks with the Taliban.[\[392\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-394) However, on 27 February, following an increase in violence, Ghani proposed unconditional peace talks with the Taliban, offering them recognition as a legal political party and the release of the Taliban prisoners. The offer was the most favorable to the Taliban since the war started. It was preceded by months of national consensus building, which found that Afghans overwhelmingly supported a negotiated end to the war.[\[393\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-395)[\[394\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-396) Two days earlier, the Taliban had called for talks with the US.[\[395\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-397) On 27 March, a conference of 20 countries in [Tashkent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tashkent "Tashkent"), [Uzbekistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbekistan "Uzbekistan"), backed the Afghan government's peace offer.[\[396\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-398) The Taliban did not publicly respond.

Following Ghani's offer of unconditional peace talks with the Taliban, a growing peace movement arose in Afghanistan during 2018, particularly following a [peace march](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_march "Peace march") by the [People's Peace Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Peace_Movement_(Afghanistan) "People's Peace Movement (Afghanistan)"),[\[397\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-TOLO_PPM_blame_foreign-399) which the Afghan media dubbed the "Helmand Peace Convoy."[\[398\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-400)[\[399\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-401) The marchers walked several hundred kilometers from Lashkar Gah in Helmand Province, through Taliban-held territory,[\[400\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-402) to Kabul. There, they met Ghani, and held sit-in protests outside the [UN Assistance Mission in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Assistance_Mission_in_Afghanistan "United Nations Assistance Mission in Afghanistan") (UNAMA) and nearby embassies.[\[401\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-403) Their efforts inspired further movements in other parts of Afghanistan.[\[402\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-404) Following the march, Ghani and the Taliban agreed a mutual, unprecedented, [ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceasefire "Ceasefire") during [Eid al-Fitr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eid_al-Fitr "Eid al-Fitr") celebrations in June. During the ceasefire, Taliban members flocked into Kabul, where they communicated with locals and state security forces. Creating a mood of both hope and fear, many civilians welcomed the Taliban and spoke about peace.[\[403\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-405) Although civilians called for the ceasefire to be made permanent, the Taliban rejected an extension and resumed fighting after the ceasefire ended on 18 June, while the Afghan government's ceasefire ended a week later.[\[404\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-406)[\[405\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-407)[\[406\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-408)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Oklahoma_National_Guard_%2825227527847%29.jpg/500px-Oklahoma_National_Guard_%2825227527847%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Oklahoma_National_Guard_(25227527847).jpg) US, British and [Afghan security forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_National_Security_Forces "Afghan National Security Forces") train together in an aerial reaction force exercise at [Camp Qargha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Qargha "Camp Qargha") in Kabul, 2018

American officials secretly met members of the Taliban's political commission in Qatar in July 2018.[\[407\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-409) In September 2018, Trump appointed [Zalmay Khalilzad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zalmay_Khalilzad "Zalmay Khalilzad") as special adviser on Afghanistan in the US State Department, with the stated goal of facilitating an intra-Afghan political peace process.[\[408\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-410) Khalilzad led further talks between the US and the Taliban in Qatar in October.[\[409\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-411) Russia hosted a separate peace talk in November between the Taliban and officials from Afghanistan's High Peace Council.[\[410\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-412) The talks in Qatar resumed in December,[\[411\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-413) though the Taliban refused to allow the Afghan government to be invited,[\[412\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-414) considering them a [puppet government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puppet_government "Puppet government") of the US.[\[413\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-BBC1-415) The Taliban spoke with Afghans, including Karzai, in Moscow in February 2019, but again these talks did not include the Afghan government.[\[414\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-416)

In July 2018, the Taliban carried out the [Darzab offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Darzab_(2018) "Battle of Darzab (2018)") and captured Darzab District, following the surrender of ISIS-K to the Afghan government. In August, the Taliban launched a series of offensives. The largest was the [Ghazni offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni_offensive "Ghazni offensive"), in which the Taliban assaulted the major city of [Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni "Ghazni") for several days, but eventually retreated.[\[415\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-417)[\[416\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-418)

On 25 January 2019, Ghani said that more than 45,000 members of the Afghan security forces had been killed since he became president in 2014. He also said that there had been fewer than 72 international casualties during the same period.[\[417\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-419) A January 2019 report by the US estimated that 53.8% of Afghan districts were government control or influence, with 33.9% contested, and 12.3% under insurgent control or influence.[\[418\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-420)

On 30 April 2019, Afghan government forces undertook clearing operations directed against both ISIS-K and the Taliban in eastern Nangarhar Province, after the two groups fought for more than a week over multiple villages in an area of illegal [talc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talc "Talc") mining. The National Directorate of Security claimed 22 ISIS-K fighters were killed and two weapons caches destroyed, while the Taliban claimed Afghan forces killed seven civilians.[\[419\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-421) On 28 July, Ghani's running mate [Amrullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amrullah_Saleh "Amrullah Saleh")'s office was attacked by a suicide bomber and a few militants. At least 20 people were killed; Saleh and 49 others were injured.[\[420\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-422)

By August, the Taliban controlled more territory than at any point since 2001.[\[421\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-423) Peace negotiations between the US and the Taliban failed in September.[\[422\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-:10-424)

On 25 February 2019, peace talks began between the Taliban and the US in Qatar, with Abdul Ghani Baradar notably present.[\[413\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-BBC1-415) After a pause in September, peace negotiations had resumed in December.[\[423\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-425) This round of talks resulted in a seven-day partial ceasefire which began on 22 February 2020.[\[424\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-426)

### 2020: US–Taliban deal, beginning of US withdrawal

Main articles: [United States–Taliban deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal "United States–Taliban deal") and [2020–2021 US troop withdrawal from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_US_troop_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan "2020–2021 US troop withdrawal from Afghanistan")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Secretary_Pompeo_Participates_in_a_Signing_Ceremony_in_Doha_%2849601220548%29.jpg/500px-Secretary_Pompeo_Participates_in_a_Signing_Ceremony_in_Doha_%2849601220548%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Secretary_Pompeo_Participates_in_a_Signing_Ceremony_in_Doha_(49601220548).jpg) US representative [Zalmay Khalilzad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zalmay_Khalilzad "Zalmay Khalilzad") (left) and Taliban representative [Abdul Ghani Baradar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Ghani_Baradar "Abdul Ghani Baradar") (right) sign the [Agreement for Bringing Peace to Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreement_for_Bringing_Peace_to_Afghanistan "Agreement for Bringing Peace to Afghanistan") on 29 February 2020

On 29 February 2020, the US and the Taliban [signed a conditional peace deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal "United States–Taliban deal") in [Doha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doha "Doha"), Qatar,[\[425\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-peace-deal-Feb29-427) that called for a prisoner exchange within ten days and was supposed to lead to US troops withdrawal from Afghanistan within 14 months.[\[426\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-proposedwithdrawal-428)[\[427\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-stillproposedwithdrawal-429) However, the Afghan government was not a party to the deal, and, in a press conference the next day, Ghani criticized the deal for being "signed behind closed doors." He said the Afghan government had "made no commitment to free 5,000 Taliban prisoners", and that such an action "is not the United States' authority", but rather Afghanistan's.[\[428\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-430)[\[429\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-431)[\[430\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-nprreject-432)[\[431\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-apreject-433)

After signing the agreement with the US, the Taliban resumed offensive operations against the Afghan army and police on 3 March, conducting attacks in Kunduz and Helmand provinces.[\[432\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-434) On 4 March, the US retaliated by launching an air strike against Taliban fighters in Helmand.[\[433\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-435) Despite the peace agreement between the US and the Taliban, insurgent attacks against Afghan security forces reportedly surged in the country. In the 45 days after the agreement (1 March to 15 April), the Taliban conducted more than 4,500 attacks in Afghanistan, which showed an increase of more than 70% as compared to the same period in the previous year.[\[434\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-re1-436) More than 900 Afghan security forces were killed in the period, up from about 520 in the same period a year earlier. Because of a significant reduction in the number of offensives and airstrikes by Afghan and US forces against the Taliban due to the agreement, Taliban casualties dropped to 610 in the period down from about 1,660 in the same period a year earlier.[\[434\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-re1-436) Meanwhile, ISIS-K continued to be a threat on its own, killing 32 people in a [mass shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6_March_2020_Kabul_shooting "6 March 2020 Kabul shooting") in Kabul on 6 March,[\[435\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-B-437) killing 25 [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh "Sikh") worshippers in Kabul on 25 March,[\[436\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-438) and a [series of attacks in May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2020_Afghanistan_attacks "May 2020 Afghanistan attacks") most notably killing 16 mothers and newborn babies in Kabul.[\[437\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-439)

On 31 March 2020, a three-person Taliban delegation arrived in Kabul to discuss the release of prisoners,[\[438\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-talibantalks-440)[\[439\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-reutersmarch31-441) the first Taliban representatives to officially visit Kabul since 2001.[\[438\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-talibantalks-440) On 7 April, the Taliban departed from the prisoner swap talks, which Taliban spokesman [Suhail Shaheen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suhail_Shaheen "Suhail Shaheen") said was unsuccessful.[\[440\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-talksfail-442)[\[441\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-prisonerelease-443) Shaheen tweeted hours later that the Taliban's negotiating team was recalled from Kabul.[\[441\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-prisonerelease-443) The Taliban failed to secure the release of any of the 15 commanders they sought to be released.[\[440\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-talksfail-442) Arguments over which prisoners to swap resulted in a delay of the planned prisoner swap.[\[440\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-talksfail-442) After a long delay due to disputes regarding prisoners' releases, the Afghan government had by August released 5,100 prisoners,[\[442\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-444) and the Taliban had released 1,000.[\[443\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-445) However, the Afghan government refused to release 400 prisoners that the Taliban requested be freed, as the prisoners were accused of serious crimes.[\[444\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-traditional_council-446) Ghani also said he lacked the constitutional authority to release them, so he convened a _loya jirga_ from 7 to 9 August to discuss the issue.[\[445\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-torelease-447) The jirga agreed to free the 400.[\[444\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-traditional_council-446) Talks between the Afghan government and the Taliban began in Doha on 12 September.[\[446\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-448)

On 22 June, Afghanistan reported its "bloodiest week in 19 years", during which 291 members of the [Afghan National Defense and Security Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_National_Defense_and_Security_Forces "Afghan National Defense and Security Forces") (ANDSF) were killed and 550 others wounded in 422 attacks carried out by the Taliban. At least 42 civilians, including women and children, were also killed and 105 others wounded.[\[447\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-449) During the week, the Taliban kidnapped 60 civilians in central [Daikundi Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daikundi_Province "Daikundi Province").[\[448\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-450)

### 2021: End of US withdrawal, last Taliban offensive

Main articles: [2021 Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive "2021 Taliban offensive") and [Fall of Kabul (2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Kabul_(2021) "Fall of Kabul (2021)")

The Taliban insurgency [intensified considerably in 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_2021_Afghanistan_attacks "List of 2021 Afghanistan attacks"), coinciding with the [withdrawal of US and allied troops from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_U.S._troop_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan "2020–2021 U.S. troop withdrawal from Afghanistan").[\[449\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-disi-451) On 6 March, Ghani expressed that his government would negotiate peace with the Taliban, discussing with them about holding new elections, and forming a government in a democratic manner.[\[450\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-452) On 13 April, the [Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden_administration "Biden administration") in the US announced that it would withdraw its remaining 2,500 troops from Afghanistan by 11 September.[\[451\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-Time_2021-453)[\[452\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-454)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/2021_Taliban_Offensive.png/500px-2021_Taliban_Offensive.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2021_Taliban_Offensive.png) A map of Afghanistan showing the 2021 Taliban offensive

The Taliban began [its last major offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive "2021 Taliban offensive") on 1 May, culminating in the [fall of Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Kabul_(2021) "Fall of Kabul (2021)"), a Taliban victory, and the end of war.[\[453\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-455)[\[454\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-fastadv1-456)[\[455\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-jul15-457) In the first three months of the offensive, the Taliban made significant territorial gains in the countryside, increasing the number of [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Afghanistan "Districts of Afghanistan") it controlled from 73 to 223.[\[456\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-lwjmap-458)

The Taliban gained control of various towns throughout June and July. On 6 August, they captured the first provincial capital of [Zaranj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaranj "Zaranj"). Over the next ten days, they swept across the country, capturing capital after capital. On 14 August, [Mazar-i-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-i-Sharif "Mazar-i-Sharif") was captured as commanders [Rashid Dostum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Rashid_Dostum "Abdul Rashid Dostum") and [Atta Nur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atta_Muhammad_Nur "Atta Muhammad Nur") fled across the border to Uzbekistan, cutting Kabul's vital northern supply route.[\[457\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-:6-459)

On 15 August, [Jalalabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalalabad "Jalalabad") fell, cutting the only remaining international route through the [Khyber Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pass "Khyber Pass").[\[457\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-:6-459) By noon, Taliban forces advanced from the [Paghman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paghman "Paghman") district reaching the gates of Kabul; Ghani discussed the city's protection with security ministers, while sources claimed a unity peace agreement with the Taliban was imminent. However, Ghani was unable to reach top officials in the interior and defense ministries, and several high-profile politicians had already hurried to the airport. By 2 p.m., the Taliban had entered the city facing no resistance; the president soon fled by helicopter from the [Presidential Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arg,_Kabul "Arg, Kabul"), and within hours, Taliban fighters were pictured at Ghani's desk in the palace.[\[458\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-460) With the virtual collapse of the republic, the Taliban declared the war over on the same day.[\[459\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)#cite_note-461)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Taliban_Humvee_in_Kabul%2C_August_2021_%28cropped%29.png/500px-Taliban_Humvee_in_Kabul%2C_August_2021_%28cropped%29.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Taliban_Humvee_in_Kabul,_August_2021_(cropped).png) Taliban fighters in Kabul, 17 August 2021

As the Taliban seized control, the need to evacuate populations vulnerable to the Taliban, including the interpreters and assistants who had worked with the coalition forces, ethnic minorities, and women, became urgent. For more than two weeks, international diplomatic, military and civilian staff, as well as Afghan civilians, were [airlifted out the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Kabul_airlift "2021 Kabul airlift") from [Hamid Karzai International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai_International_Airport "Hamid Karzai International Airport").
